Verified end to end: `net from`+`net install` against both the local repository path and (after pushing) the live GitHub-hosted `stata.toc` install every chunk of every package cleanly, and a full `nwinstall, all` run into a fresh, empty `PLUS` directory materializes 115 `.ado`/120 `.sthlp` files with `_rc == 0`. |
| **Real, unrelated bug found while verifying the install loop above: the same `_rc`-staleness class already fixed repeatedly elsewhere this session, now in `nwinstall.ado`'s own new loop** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | The loop's own natural exit condition is an *expected*, `capture`-swallowed failure (chunk `i` genuinely doesn't exist) - without a reset, that left a misleading nonzero `_rc` standing as `nwinstall`'s own apparent result even when every real chunk installed successfully. Fixed with the same `capture confirm number 1` idiom used for `nwsync.ado`/`nwplot.ado` earlier this session. |
| **Real, higher-impact gap found on the very first genuinely clean-environment test: the compiled Mata library was never part of the package manifest at all** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Every command's actual implementation lives in Mata classes/functions compiled into `lib/lnwcommands.mlib` - `_pkg_ado.txt` never referenced it, so a fresh `net install` (every `.ado`/`.sthlp` file successfully fetched, `_rc == 0` throughout) still failed on the very first real command (`nws_create() not found`, r3499). Fixed by adding `f lib/lnwcommands.mlib` to `_pkg_ado.txt` - confirmed directly (an isolated repro before touching the real package) that `net install`'s own file mechanism correctly fetches from a server-side subdirectory and lands the file in the destination's standard first-letter-bucketed `mlib` location, exactly where Mata's own library search already looks, with no further configuration needed. Verified end to end against the live, pushed GitHub repository (not just locally) with a completely clean `PLUS` directory - `net install` four packages, then `nwset`/`nwbetween` immediately work, `_rc == 0`. One practical gotcha worth recording: GitHub's raw-content CDN caches for up to 5 minutes (`cache-control: max-age=300`), so a push doesn't take effect for live verification instantly - a real, if minor, thing to know about this distribution channel. |
| Full `cscripts/` sweep (dev mode, 141 files, after every fix above) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | No regressions beyond the one pre-existing, unrelated baseline failure (`nwimport`'s third-party host). `_nwdeploy.ado` itself has no `cscripts/` coverage (a build/release tool, not a network-analysis command) - verified instead by running it end to end against the live repository and inspecting every regenerated file directly. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 100: `nwclustering`/`nw2clustering` auto-detect valued networks (audit of the rest of the package found no other gaps)

User-requested: "Commands should detect themselves whether network is two-mode, values or such." Audited every command with a `measure(string)`-style binary-vs-weighted option (`nwclustering`, `nw2clustering`, `nwcommunity`, `nwconcor`, `nwcoreperiphery`, `nwmodularity`, `nwspectral`) and every command handling both one-mode and two-mode networks (`nw2degree`, `nw2project`, `nwclustering`, `nwdegree`, `nwergm`, `nwtoedge`, `nwuse`, plus their shared `nw_syntax`/`nw_datasync`/`nw_name` plumbing).

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Two-mode auto-detection audit | ✅ (no gap found) | n/a | n/a | n/a | Every command that needs to branch on two-mode-ness already does, via the same `is2mode`/`` `is2mode' `` convention (`nwclustering`/`nwdegree` both auto-detect and transparently redirect to their `nw2*` counterpart; `nw_syntax` populates `is2mode` for every caller uniformly). `nwset` itself is the one place a caller must state `bipartite`/`twomode` explicitly - unavoidable, since two-mode-ness genuinely can't be inferred before the network exists (it depends on what the raw rows/columns *mean*, not just their shape). |
| **Real gap found and fixed: `nwclustering.ado`/`nw2clustering.ado` always defaulted `measure` to `"binary"`, ignoring the network's own valued/unvalued state** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | n/a (behavior-only default change, no syntax change) | Every other measure-style command in the package (`nwcommunity`/`nwconcor`/`nwcoreperiphery`/`nwmodularity`/`nwspectral`) already auto-selects a valued-appropriate default when the caller doesn't specify one explicitly; these two were the only holdouts, silently dichotomizing a valued network's clustering coefficient by default with no indication anything was discarded. Fixed to default to `"arithmetic"` (a standard weighted clustering coefficient formula, already one of the command's own existing choices) when the network `is_valued()` and the caller passed nothing - `"binary"` remains available as an explicit choice and is still the default for a genuinely unvalued network. `nwclustering.ado` specifically also guards on `` `directed' `` in this same check: a directed+valued network still defaults to `"binary"`, since a weighted measure is undefined for directed networks in this command (its own pre-existing guard) - auto-defaulting to `"arithmetic"` there would have turned a previously-working call into a hard error by default, a regression rather than a fix. `nw2clustering.ado` additionally needed its measure-default logic moved to *after* its own `nw_syntax` call - the `valued` local it now reads wasn't populated yet at the check's original position. |
| Full `cscripts/` sweep (dev mode, 141 files) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | No regressions beyond the one pre-existing, unrelated baseline failure (`nwimport`'s third-party host). |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 101: cross-platform native plugins actually build now (they never had)

User-requested: "make sure that we run on different operating systems as well... can github help here?" A complete-looking GitHub Actions workflow (`.github/workflows/build-plugins.yml`, from unit 87) already existed for exactly this, but had a real bug that meant it had never once executed.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Real bug found: the workflow's trigger only matched `[main, master]`, but this project's actual active branch is `develop`** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Confirmed directly via `gh run list` before touching anything: zero runs ever, despite `native/*.c` changing across many commits on `develop` since the workflow was added. Fixed by adding `develop` to both the push trigger and the `commit-binaries` job's own branch gate. |
| **Real bug found on the first genuine run: the Windows job's hardcoded `vcvarsall.bat` path doesn't exist on the actual `windows-latest` runner** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `"the system cannot find the path specified"` / `"'cl' is not recognized"` - the workflow's own two hardcoded guesses (VS 2022 Enterprise/Community) matched neither the runner's real installation. Fixed with `ilammy/msvc-dev-cmd@v1`, the standard GitHub Action for locating whatever MSVC installation a runner actually has, rather than guessing a fixed path - a class of bug only ever discoverable by actually running the workflow, which unit 87 could not do (no Windows runner in that authoring environment). |
| **Result: all three platforms now build and commit successfully** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | macOS, Windows, and Linux plugin binaries (`ergm_mcmc`/`nwgraph`, 2 kernels × 3 platforms = 6 files) now exist in `lib/plugins/`, auto-committed by the `commit-binaries` job. Before this unit, only macOS had a committed binary (built locally); Windows and Linux users were silently falling back to the pure-Mata path for both `nwergm`'s MCMC backend and `nwbetween`'s native betweenness - not broken, just unaccelerated, and now fixed. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 102: `vertex_connectivity()` was O(n^2)/O(n^2)-per-call and catastrophically slow - fixed with a genuine algorithmic rewrite

User-requested performance study surfaced this directly: `nwkcomponents` on a mere 100-node, average-degree-10 random graph took **66 seconds** in the very first benchmark run - the single slowest result by two orders of magnitude out of ~45 commands tested. The user's own first instinct was to consider a native C graph library (igraph specifically) for this; investigated and found the actual cause was algorithmic, not interpreter overhead - a native port of the same bad algorithm would still have been quadratic, just with a smaller constant, and igraph itself is already ruled out for this project on GPL-licensing grounds (`docs/NATIVE_GRAPH_LIBRARIES.md`, unit 95). Fixed the algorithm instead.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Root cause #1: `vertex_connectivity()`/`min_vertex_cutset()` searched literally every non-adjacent pair (O(n^2) max-flow calls) to find the graph's global vertex connectivity** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Replaced with `fast_min_cut_search()`, implementing the Esfahanian & Hakimi (1984) refinement of Even's algorithm: pick a minimum-degree vertex v0 (degree delta), check local connectivity from v0 to its non-neighbors (O(delta) calls) plus every non-adjacent pair among v0's own neighbors (O(delta^2) calls) - O(delta^2) total, independent of n, versus the old O(n^2). A huge win for sparse graphs (delta << n), the overwhelmingly common case for this package's own commands. `min_vertex_cutset()` was doing the identical O(n^2) search a second time (to extract the actual cutset, not just its size) - refactored to share the same fast search, with a direct (no-max-flow-needed) fallback for the case where the minimum is the plain degree bound itself. |
| **Root cause #2, found once #1 stopped dominating: `maxflow_vertex_split()` rebuilt an O(n^2) dense capacity matrix from `adj` on every single call** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Even with O(delta^2) calls instead of O(n^2), each call was still paying O(n^2) just to construct its own capacity matrix via an interpreted double loop - the actual dominant cost once #1 alone still left n=1000 timing out past 120 seconds. Fixed in two steps: (a) replaced the interpreted double-loop construction with a single vectorized Mata assignment; (b) factored construction into `build_vertexsplit_basecap()`, called ONCE per `fast_min_cut_search()` invocation and shared (via a Mata pointer) across all of that invocation's O(delta^2) queries, each of which now just flips two cells to mark its own (s,t) query and restores them afterward, instead of rebuilding the whole matrix every time. `bfs_augment()` (the max-flow search's own inner loop) had the same class of issue - scanning a full dense row per dequeued node via an interpreted loop - fixed with a vectorized row-extraction + `selectindex()` instead. |
| **Correctness, verified extensively given this is a nontrivial graph-theory rewrite** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | The exact pre-fix brute-force algorithm was kept (test-only, not used anywhere in production) as a reference oracle and cross-checked against the new fast algorithm across 442 random graphs per run (sizes 4-30, densities 0.15-0.75) plus complete graphs, empty graphs, and disconnected two-clique graphs at multiple sizes - run three times independently (1,326 total comparisons), zero mismatches. Additionally verified against hand-computable closed-form cases (K5's kappa=4, a 5-node path's kappa=1, a 5-cycle's kappa=2, two disjoint edges' kappa=0). Permanent regression guard added: `cscripts/test_vertex_connectivity.do`. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | `nwkcomponents` on the same 100-node/avg-degree-10 test network: **66 seconds → 0.029 seconds** (~2,275x). At 1,000 nodes (same avg degree): previously did not complete within 2 minutes at all; now **17.5 seconds** - a real, large improvement, though the remaining cost (a dense max-flow representation of a fundamentally sparse graph) means this specific command still does not scale as well as the package's genuinely sparse-native commands (`nwcomponents`/`nwdegree`/etc., benchmarked at 100,000 nodes elsewhere). A fully sparse (adjacency-list-based) max-flow rewrite would close this remaining gap but is a larger, separate undertaking - noted as a Pending item below rather than attempted under the same time pressure that found this bug, given the correctness stakes of a delicate flow algorithm. |
| Full `cscripts/` sweep (both modes, 142 files, after every fix above) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | No regressions beyond the one pre-existing, unrelated baseline failure (`nwimport`'s third-party host). `lib/lnwcommands.mlib` rebuilt clean. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 103: full performance benchmark study at N = 100/1,000/10,000

User-requested: "stress-test every single command on a network of 100, 1000 and 10000 size. Give complete overview of timings and performance here. We will need this for another performance optimization. But I also want to report these results in a stata journal article." Full report: `docs/PERFORMANCE_BENCHMARKS.md`; reusable script: `dev/benchmark_suite.do`; raw data: `dev/benchmark_results.csv`.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Benchmark harness covering ~45 commands across 8 categories at 3 sizes | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Standardized sparse random test network per size (avg degree held at ~10 across sizes, matching `docs/SPARSE_BACKEND.md`'s own convention); reduced rep counts for permutation-based commands; single-rep timing (a documented methodology tradeoff, not a median). |
| **The benchmark run itself found and fixed the vertex_connectivity() catastrophic-slowness bug** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | See unit 102 above - discovered directly from this benchmark's own first N=100 run (`nwkcomponents` at 66 seconds), not a separate investigation. |
| Full results table across all three tiers, with exclusions documented and reasoned | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | See `docs/PERFORMANCE_BENCHMARKS.md`'s own full table. ~20 commands not reached at n=10,000 due to time constraints (explicitly distinguished from confirmed scaling problems) - `dev/benchmark_suite.do` is ready to extend and complete this in a future pass. |
| **Two more real, previously-undiscovered bugs found during the benchmark's own setup validation** | ✅ (documented, not fixed) | n/a | n/a | ✅ | `nw2clustering.ado` fails with a reshape error (r9) on *any* bipartite network, including tiny ones - a malformed `i()`-varlist string-construction bug, not an artifact of this suite's own test networks. `nwmixing.ado` fails a precondition check (r111, "variable X_nwego not found") even against a plain, freshly-`nwload`ed attribute variable - its own internal `nwtoedge ..., egovars()/altervars()` call doesn't materialize what it immediately expects to exist. Neither fixed here (out of scope for a benchmark-driven pass); both added to Pending below. |
| **The single slowest *completing* command found: `nwcoreperiphery`, 255 seconds at n=1,000** (up from 0.175s at n=100 - a ~1,450x slowdown for a 10x increase in n) | ✅ (found, not fixed) | n/a | n/a | ✅ | Not previously known - no existing test or prior benchmark caught this, since the command completes without erroring. Recommended as the top priority for the next optimization pass in `docs/PERFORMANCE_BENCHMARKS.md`. |
| A cluster of commands confirmed dense-matrix-dependent (`nwcloseness`/`nwburt`/`nwconstraint`/`nwissymmetric`/`nwsimilar`/`nwdissimilar`/`nwqap` via `nwtomata`; `nwtriads` via `get_matrix_unvalued()` directly) | ✅ (found, not fixed) | n/a | n/a | ✅ | A candidate list for a future systematic sparse-migration pass, given the pattern (and this harmonisation phase's own fix template from `nwevcent`/`vertex_connectivity()`) is now well-established. |
| `nwgeodesic` did not complete at n=10,000 in this study | ✅ (found, not diagnosed) | n/a | n/a | ✅ | Flagged as a genuine anomaly, not assumed to share the dense-matrix cause above - the underlying sparse BFS distance family is separately proven at 100,000+ nodes in `docs/SPARSE_BACKEND.md`. **Fixed** in harmonisation unit 106: a queue-concatenation antipattern in `bfs_hopdist_from()`, not a memory issue - now completes in 107 seconds. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 104: `nwclustering` was calling nothing - a fully-built, already-sparse Mata implementation existed and was never wired up

User-requested direct follow-up to unit 103's own benchmark report: "now we need to work to optimize the code to get all these commands to run in reasonable time for 10k nodes... feel free to use C plugins or other libraries if needed." `nwclustering` (0.35s/6.5s/459s at n=100/1,000/10,000) was next on that report's own priority list. No C plugin was needed - the fix was entirely architectural.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Root cause: `nwclustering.ado` never called `calculate_clustering()` at all** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `calculate_clustering()` in `unw_core.do` already implemented sparse neighbor-based clustering-coefficient computation across all 5 measure modes, with its own code comment explicitly noting it was dead code ("no shipped .ado file calls calculate_clustering() today"). `nwclustering.ado` instead ran its own independent, much slower Stata-level pipeline: `nwtoedge` + three `reshape wide`/`reshape long` round-trips + two `merge m:m` string-keyed joins, enumerating every length-2 path in the graph via dataset operations - the actual, confirmed cause of the 459-second n=10,000 time (Stata's `reshape`/`merge` per-observation overhead does not scale to tens of thousands of edges). Fixed by rewriting `nwclustering.ado` to call `calculate_clustering()` directly (mapping its own `measure()` string to the function's existing 0-4 mode convention), storing its returned per-node column straight into the `generate()` variable via `st_store()` and deriving `r(cluster_avg)`/`r(cluster_global)` the same way `nwcoreperiphery.ado` already does for its own Mata-computed results - no new algorithm, just wiring an existing one up. |
| **`calculate_clustering()` itself still touched the dense matrix** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Before this unit, every mode's innermost pairwise lookup went through `(*get_matrix_unvalued())[a,b]`/`(*get_matrix())[a,b]`, forcing `ensure_dense_built()` to materialize the full N-by-N matrix even though neighbor *enumeration* was already sparse. Replaced with `has_edge()`/`edge_weight()` (the same safe "check `has_edge()` before reading `edge_weight()`" pattern `nwevcent`'s own sparse migration established in unit 97) - the function no longer touches the dense matrix at all. |
| **A real, independent correctness bug found and fixed during validation, not introduced by this unit** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | An initial sparse-migration draft of mode 0 (binary) paired every two elements of `union(out-neighbors(i), in-neighbors(i))` against each other, checking `has_edge()` in both directions - mathematically equivalent to the *actually shipping* reshape-pipeline's own directed-triad definition only for undirected networks (where in-neighbors == out-neighbors == all-neighbors), and silently wrong for directed ones. Caught by `cscripts/test_nwtriads.do`'s pre-existing directed-network case (`r(transitivity)` came back `.5` instead of the asserted `1`) - not by any test written for this unit itself. Root-caused by hand-tracing the old reshape pipeline's actual semantics (it pairs one in-neighbor with one out-neighbor of the focal node - the directed two-path `a -> i -> b` - and checks whether the direct shortcut `a -> b` closes it) and confirming the discrepancy by running the *actual pre-fix* `nwclustering.ado` (preserved from git history under a renamed program) side by side with the new implementation on the exact failing network. Fixed by replacing the union-based pairing with the correct in-neighbor x out-neighbor cross-product (the same idiom `calculate_brokerage()` already uses elsewhere in this file for its own directed two-path enumeration), which reduces to exactly the old union-based formula for undirected networks (verified both algebraically and empirically) while matching the true shipped behavior for directed ones. |
| **Correctness, verified against the actual pre-fix implementation before trusting the rewrite** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | The pre-fix `nwclustering.ado` was preserved (via `git show HEAD:nwclustering.ado`, loaded under a renamed program) and run side by side with the new implementation across: the pre-existing `cscripts/test_nwclustering.do` hand-verified Opsahl & Panzarasa (2009) reference values (binary/geometric/minimum/maximum modes, all passing with `reldif < 1E-8`) plus an added direct check of arithmetic mode (exact match to 14 decimal places); a 60-node random undirected network (all 5 modes, exact match, zero missing-value mismatches); a 40-node and a 10,000-node random *directed* network (binary mode, exact match) - the last of these being the specific case the discovered bug above affected. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | n=10,000, undirected: **459.3 seconds -> 1.6-1.7 seconds** (~275x). n=10,000, directed: **1.7 seconds** (previously not separately benchmarked, but subject to the same reshape-pipeline cost). |
| Scoped regression sweep (risk-based, not a full suite sweep - see `docs/CERTIFICATION.md`'s own tiered-certification convention) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | Blast radius traced via direct grep for actual `nwclustering` callers (not just doc "See also" mentions): `nwgenerate.ado` (its `clustering()` sub-function) and `nwtriads.ado` (its "Transitivity" figure). `test_nwclustering.do`/`test_nwgenerate.do`/`test_nwtriads.do`/`test_nw2clustering.do` (the last as a quick drive-by given the similar name, unaffected) all pass clean in both dev mode and production mode (against the rebuilt `lib/lnwcommands.mlib`). |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 105: `nwcoreperiphery` was O(n) fitness evaluations away from O(1) ones - fixed with an incremental-update rewrite

Direct continuation of unit 104's own "next" item, per the same user directive ("optimize the code to get all these commands to run in reasonable time for 10k nodes... feel free to use C plugins or other libraries if needed"). `nwcoreperiphery` was unit 103's own single slowest *completing* command (255s at n=1,000) and had not been reached at n=10,000 at all. No C plugin needed here either.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Root cause: the greedy local search re-evaluated the ENTIRE O(n^2) fitness correlation from scratch on every single candidate flip** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `CorePeriphery()`'s search tries flipping each node's core/periphery status in turn (n candidates per sweep, up to `maxiter` sweeps), scoring each candidate by calling `nw_cp_fitness()` twice (before/after) - and each call recomputed the Pearson correlation between the network and the "ideal" core-periphery pattern over *all* n(n-1) off-diagonal dyads from scratch. That is O(n^2) work per candidate, O(n) candidates per sweep - O(n^3) per sweep, the confirmed cause of 255s at n=1,000. Fixed by observing that flipping one node i's core status only changes the "expected pattern" value at dyads between i and the *periphery* nodes (a core-anything dyad's expected value doesn't depend on which side is core) - so the correlation's sufficient statistics (the sums needed for Pearson's r) can be updated in O(n) per candidate instead of recomputed in O(n^2), turning O(n^3) per sweep into O(n^2). The one-time O(n^2) cost of seeding those sums for the initial core assignment, and one final exact O(n^2) `nw_cp_fitness()` call to report the final fitness (avoiding any concern about floating-point drift accumulated over many incremental updates), are both paid once, not per candidate. |
| **A real, subtle behavioral quirk in the pre-existing algorithm, found by cross-validation and deliberately preserved rather than silently fixed** | ✅ (preserved) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | A zero-variance dyad pattern (e.g. an all-periphery or all-core state) makes Mata's `correlation()` return missing, and Mata's missing sorts as *greater than any real number* (confirmed directly: `. > 5` is `1`) - so the pre-existing search actually treats a degenerate fitness as unconditionally better than any real one, and once it falls into a degenerate state it can never leave (nothing can then compare greater than missing). An initial draft of this rewrite used a "treat zero variance as fitness 0" fallback instead - more sensible in isolation, but it silently changes which local optimum the search converges to. Caught by a 300-network deterministic cross-check against the actual pre-fix algorithm (extracted from git history under renamed functions), not by theoretical review - two networks (n=7 and n=4 in that sweep) exposed the divergence directly (old converged to a degenerate all-periphery state with missing fitness; the "fixed" fallback converged to a different, non-degenerate optimum). Fixed by using an explicit missing value in the same zero-variance case, so Mata's own `>` operator reproduces the exact same quirk. |
| **Correctness, verified against the actual pre-fix algorithm before trusting the rewrite** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | The pre-fix `CorePeriphery()`/`nw_cp_fitness()` were preserved (via `git show HEAD:unw_core.do`, loaded under renamed functions in the same Mata session) and run side by side with the new implementation: 300 deterministically-generated valued networks (n=4-30, ~25% density) plus 500 further genuinely-random valued networks (n=5-40, ~30% density) - core assignment vector and reported fitness checked for exact equality on every single one (zero mismatches across both sweeps, including the two degenerate-fitness edge cases the quirk-preservation fix above was needed for). |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | n=100: 0.175s -> 0.003s. **n=1,000: 255s -> 0.094s (~2,700x)**. n=10,000 (not previously reached at all in unit 103's study): **10.9 seconds** - now completes, and in what this package's own benchmark report would call reasonable time for a combinatorial local-search command. |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | Direct grep found no other `.ado` actually calls `nwcoreperiphery` (only doc "See also" mentions in `nwbrokerage.ado`/`nwclique.ado`/`nwclustering.ado`/`nw2clustering.ado`) and `nwgenerate.ado` has no `coreperiphery` sub-function - blast radius is the command itself. `cscripts/test_nwcoreperiphery.do` passes clean in both dev mode and production mode (against the rebuilt `lib/lnwcommands.mlib`). |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 106: `nwgeodesic`'s own "did not complete at n=10,000" anomaly (unit 103) was a queue-concatenation antipattern inside an otherwise-sparse BFS, not a memory blow-up

Direct follow-up to unit 103's own flagged anomaly: `nwgeodesic` did not complete at n=10,000 despite the underlying sparse BFS distance family being separately proven at 100,000+ nodes in `docs/SPARSE_BACKEND.md` - explicitly not assumed to share the dense-matrix cause of the commands fixed in units 104/105. Investigated directly (per the user's "optimize the code... for 10k nodes" directive) rather than left as a documented-only anomaly.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Root cause, confirmed by direct observation before touching any code**: `bfs_hopdist_from()` rebuilt each BFS level's frontier via `newqueue = newqueue \ nb` - repeated vertical matrix concatenation, reallocating and copying the whole growing vector on every neighbor batch appended within a level | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Confirmed NOT a memory issue first: a live `nwgeodesic bignet` run at n=10,000 was watched directly via `ps` for several minutes - RSS stayed flat (~2.5GB) while CPU stayed pegged at 100%, ruling out the "just an inherent O(n^2)-memory all-pairs-distance materialization" explanation that would otherwise have been the easy, low-risk conclusion (and the one initially considered, given `nwgeodesic`'s own output genuinely does require an O(n^2) dense result). The actual cost is algorithmic: for a random sparse graph, most nodes are discovered within 2-3 BFS hops, so a handful of levels carry large frontiers, and rebuilding each one via repeated concatenation makes that level's own cost quadratic in its size rather than linear - defeating the O(n+m)-per-source design the surrounding sparse architecture (`neighbors()`/CSR) was built to provide. Fixed with the standard array-based-queue BFS: one preallocated length-n vector (a node is enqueued at most once) with a read pointer and a write pointer, zero reallocation. |
| **Correctness, verified against the actual pre-fix algorithm before trusting the rewrite** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | The pre-fix `bfs_hopdist_from()` (via the file's own git-history version, loaded as a full separate Mata session) was run end-to-end through `nwgeodesic` + `nwtomata` on a 200-node undirected and a 150-node directed random network, exported to CSV, and diffed byte-for-byte against the same run against the new implementation - identical on both. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | `nwgeodesic` at n=10,000: previously did not complete (this study's own time constraints, likely 8+ minutes); now **107 seconds**. `nwcloseness` (which wraps `nwgeodesic` + `nwtomata` + a row-sum) at n=10,000: **108 seconds** - both now genuinely complete, though still bound by the inherent O(n^2) size of an all-pairs-distance result at this n; not attempted further in this unit (see Pending below for the fully sparse, matrix-free farness computation `nwcloseness` specifically could use instead). |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | Blast radius traced via direct grep for every caller of `calculate_distances_bfs()`/`bfs_hopdist_from()`/the shared `calculate_distances(0,"brute")` path: `nwgeodesic` (direct), `nwcloseness` (via `nwgeodesic`), `nwnclique.ado` (`calculate_nclique()`), `nwaltergen.ado` (`calculate_alterstat_hop()`), `nwbridges.ado`/`nwpath.ado` (`calculate_distances_without()`/`get_path()`). `test_nwgeodesic.do`/`test_nwcloseness.do`/`test_nwnclique.do`/`test_nwnclan.do`/`test_nwaltergen.do`/`test_nwbridges.do`/`test_nwpath.do` all pass clean in both dev mode and production mode. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 107: `BronKerbosch()` pivoting added (a genuine, verified improvement) - but `nwclique` at n=10,000 needs a deeper, still-open architectural fix, not claimed as solved

`nwclique` was the other command flagged in unit 103 as not completing at n=10,000 (killed after 8+ minutes). Investigated directly per the user's "optimize... for 10k nodes" directive. Unlike units 104-106, this one is reported honestly as **partially resolved**: a real, verified improvement was made and kept, but the n=10,000 sparse-graph case specifically is not yet fixed and needs a larger rewrite than this unit attempted.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Diagnosis: confirmed a genuine search-tree blow-up, not a combinatorial-output blow-up (ruling out the "same as nwkplex" explanation before assuming it)** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | ✅ | Directly measured the actual maximal-clique *count* at n=1,000 (173) and n=2,000 (163) - essentially flat, not growing with n - while wall-clock time went 1.4s -> 13.4s for that same roughly-constant output, and n=10,000 hit 28GB+ RSS before being killed. This rules out "the graph genuinely has an astronomical number of maximal cliques" (nwkplex's own, different, inherent k>=2 finding) and points at the *search tree* explored to find those ~170 cliques, not the cliques themselves. |
| **Added standard Bron-Kerbosch pivoting (Tomita, Tanaka & Takahashi 2006)** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `BronKerbosch()`'s own header comment previously defended the plain unpivoted version as sufficient for "this package's own target network scale" - no longer true now the package targets n=10,000. Added the standard pivot-vertex optimization (branch only on P minus the neighbors of a vertex chosen from P union X to maximize overlap with P), a textbook, provably clique-set-preserving pruning of redundant branches. Verified via cross-check against the pre-fix unpivoted algorithm (extracted from git history) on 400 random graphs (n=3-25, ~20% density) plus 300 further denser ones (n=3-18, ~45% density) - comparing the exact *set* of returned maximal cliques (not just counts, canonicalized as sorted node-id strings per clique), zero mismatches. A first cross-check attempt showed 400/400 "mismatches" that turned out to be a test-harness bug (passing the same R/P/X matrix variables into two sequential function calls triggered a Mata copy-on-write aliasing interaction) - traced and fixed in the test, not the production code, before trusting the result. |
| **Measured effect: real on moderately dense graphs, negligible on this benchmark's very sparse one** | ✅ (honestly reported) | ✅ | n/a | ✅ | n=60 at ~40% density: 0.029s -> 0.007s (~4x) - pivoting working as designed. n=1,000/2,000 at the benchmark's own avg-degree-10 density: 1.4s->1.8s and 13.4s->16.0s respectively - *no improvement, slightly worse* - and n=10,000 still hit 28GB+ RSS and was killed, unchanged from before. Root cause understood, not just observed: for a very sparse graph, average degree is far smaller than n, so even the *best possible* pivot only ever excludes about (average degree) nodes from branching at the top of the recursion, where P still has close to n candidates - pivoting cannot meaningfully shrink that top-level branching factor here. The deeper, actual cost driver at this scale is that P/X/R are dense 0/1 indicator vectors over *all* n nodes at every recursion level, so even a call whose true local candidate set is tiny (bounded by degree, deep in the tree) still pays O(n) per vector operation (`adj[v,.]`, `P :& Nv`, etc.) - fixing that needs representing P/X/R as sparse index lists local to each node's actual neighbourhood, a materially larger rewrite of a correctness-critical recursive algorithm than this unit attempted. |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | Direct grep for callers of `BronKerbosch()`: `calculate_cliques()` (`nwclique`) and `calculate_nclique()` (`nwnclique`/`nwnclan`) - `nwkplex`/`nwcohesion` use their own separate KPlex()/cohesion logic, not `BronKerbosch()` itself, but were tested anyway given their doc-header cross-references to it. `test_nwclique.do`/`test_nwnclique.do`/`test_nwnclan.do`/`test_nwkplex.do`/`test_nwcohesion.do` all pass clean in both dev mode and production mode - the pivoting change is a safe, verified keep even though it does not by itself close the n=10,000 gap. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 108: `nwkcomponents`/`nwcohesion` no longer hit an unbounded memory blow-up at n=10,000 - fully sparse vertex-connectivity search, edge-list-native recursion, plus a correctness-preserving early-exit

Direct continuation of the user's "optimize... for 10k nodes... feel free to use C plugins or other libraries if needed" directive, closing the residual gap unit 102 itself flagged (a dense (2n)x(2n) max-flow representation) and the further gap this same investigation found underneath it. This was the single largest remaining item from the whole optimization pass - three separate, independently-verified fixes were needed, found by direct measurement at each stage rather than assumed from the first diagnosis.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Fix 1: sparse (edge-list) vertex-split max-flow, replacing the dense (2n)x(2n) capacity matrix** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `build_vertexsplit_basecap()`/`maxflow_vertex_split_shared()` (unit 102) cut `fast_min_cut_search()`'s CALL COUNT to O(delta^2), but each call still did O(n^2) work against a dense matrix - confirmed directly as the reason `nwkcomponents` still hit 28GB+/9.5GB+ RAM and was killed before finishing at n=10,000. Added `build_vertexsplit_sparse()`/`maxflow_sparse_query()`/`bfs_augment_sparse()`: the identical vertex-split max-flow graph represented as parallel edge arrays (edgeTo/edgeCap/edgeRev, a linked-list adjacency via headEdge/nextEdge - the standard adjacency-list max-flow representation) instead of a dense matrix, built once and shared across all of one search's O(delta^2) queries via pointers, exactly mirroring the "build once, patch 2 cells per query" discipline already established for the dense version. Verified byte-for-byte identical flow values against the still-unmodified dense implementation (kept as `cscripts/test_vertex_connectivity.do`'s own reference oracle) across 500+587 random graphs, all non-adjacent pairs checked, zero mismatches. |
| **A real, independent bug found and fixed while building this: Mata's `a::b` range operator returns a DESCENDING sequence when `a>b`, not empty** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Confirmed directly (`6::5` evaluates to `(6,5)`, a 2-element vector, not `J(1,0,0)`). A CSR neighbor-list slice (`colidx[(rowptr[v]+1)::rowptr[v+1]]`) for the minimum-degree vertex - which can genuinely have degree 0 (an isolated node) - silently pulled two bogus `colidx` entries into its own "neighbor set" instead of correctly finding none, without an explicit `rowptr[v]+1 <= rowptr[v+1]` guard. Caught by a 500-test cross-validation run against the dense reference (not by inspection) once sparse graphs with isolated nodes were included in the test mix. |
| **Fix 2: `KComponents()` is now a thin one-time-setup wrapper; the entire recursion (`KComponentsEdges()`) is edge-list/CSR-native, never touching a dense matrix at any level** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Fix 1 alone barely moved the needle for a graph needing several SEQUENTIAL small cuts before its "remaining" portion stabilizes (measured: 79.9s -> 72.6s at n=5,000) - direct instrumentation showed why: `KComponents()` re-derived a fresh dense n_level-by-n_level submatrix (`origadj[idx,idx]`) and re-scanned it from scratch at EVERY recursion level, paying that near-full-size O(n^2) cost once per cut (~7 such levels observed). Fixed by extracting a symmetric edge list AND a CSR (rowptr/colidx) from the dense input exactly ONCE, in the wrapper, then filtering/renumbering that fixed-size edge list to the current node subset at each level via a single vectorized O(m_full) pass (m_full = the ORIGINAL graph's total edge count, not the shrinking-but-still-O(n_level^2) submatrix cost it replaces) - both the vertex-connectivity search and the "split remaining into connected components" BFS (the latter itself already vectorized in a prior pass of this same investigation, now walking the CSR instead of a dense row) now cost time proportional to the current level's own edges. Verified via 1,700+ cross-checks against `KComponentsOld` (the pre-refactor version, extracted from git history) across random and deliberately multi-weak-point graphs (several distinct cutpoints of different sizes, designed to stress exactly the scenario a partial/approximate fix could get wrong), comparing the exact returned *set* of k-components, not just counts. |
| **A genuinely new, previously-undocumented Mata constraint found the hard way: identifiers are capped at 32 characters** | ✅ (documented) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | A first-choice function name, `build_edgelist_and_csr_from_dense` (33 characters), compiled with `'<name>' found where name expected` at its own definition line - no indication length was the issue. Bisected to a minimal repro (identical code, renaming the function was the only change) before finding the exact boundary: 32 characters compiles clean, 33 fails identically. Renamed to `build_edgelist_csr_dense` (24 characters); all of this unit's other new identifiers were also audited and are under the limit. |
| **Fix 3 (the largest single win): correctness-preserving early-exit, once the true remaining bottleneck was isolated by direct per-call instrumentation** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Fix 2 alone still left n=5,000 at 72.6s - direct instrumentation (wall-clock per recursion-level call) found the actual cause: `fast_min_cut_search_edges()`'s own step "(2), v0 to every vertex it is NOT adjacent to" is genuinely O(n-1-delta) max-flow queries, not O(delta) as this function's own header comment (inherited unmodified from harmonisation unit 102) claimed - a claim that happened to hold for unit 102's own benchmark graphs (delta was close to n there) but is false in general: a degree-1 vertex has ~n-2 non-neighbors, all queried, with no early termination. A single such call, on a near-isolated-node subgraph, took 9-24 SECONDS by itself. Fixed by observing that `KComponentsEdges()` only ever needs to know whether connectivity is below its own fixed target `k` - not the exact global minimum - and only needs SOME (s,t) pair achieving a cut below `k`, not the smallest one: added an optional `target` argument to `fast_min_cut_search_edges()` that stops the search the instant any query returns a flow strictly below it, leaving every other existing/future caller (which does not pass `target') getting the exact global minimum exactly as before. |
| **Proved, not just tested, that using a non-minimal (merely sub-target) cutset instead of the graph's true minimum cannot break the recursive decomposition's correctness** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | The concern (worth taking seriously before trusting an early-exit on a correctness-critical recursive algorithm): does Kanevsky's decomposition require the *globally minimum* cutset at each step, or does *any* valid vertex separator smaller than the target `k` suffice? Short argument: if a candidate k-connected subset S' had members on both sides of ANY cutset C with `|C| < k` (minimal or not), then C restricted to S' would itself be a separator of S' with size `< k`, contradicting S' being k-connected - so S' must lie entirely on one side of every sub-k cut, regardless of whether that cut is the smallest possible one. This means any valid (s,t) separator found via early-exit preserves the decomposition's correctness. Verified empirically anyway, at the same rigor as an unproven change would get: 1,700+ exact-match cross-checks against `KComponentsOld` (see Fix 2's own row), including a dedicated 300-test batch built specifically with multiple distinct weak points of different sizes (several dense clusters joined by bridges of varying width) to try to trigger the exact "settles for a larger-than-necessary but still valid cut" scenario the theoretical concern describes - zero mismatches. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | `nwkcomponents` at n=5,000: 79.9s (fix 1 only) -> 72.6s (+ fix 2) -> **22.9s** (+ fix 3, early-exit). At n=10,000: previously did not complete at all (28GB+/9.5GB+ RAM, killed) -> **103.5 seconds**, genuinely bounded and completing. The remaining cost is understood, not mysterious: *confirming* a subgraph's connectivity already meets the target still requires the full O(n) exhaustive scan (early-exit cannot help disprove a lower bound, only prove one) - a further, lower-risk buffer-reuse optimization (a timestamp-based "visited" array avoiding repeated O(N) allocation across the O(n) queries) was identified but not attempted in this unit, given the scale of improvement already banked and the correctness stakes of continuing to modify this code under time pressure; left as a documented Pending item. |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | `test_vertex_connectivity.do` (the dense reference-oracle test, unmodified), `test_nwkcomponents.do`, `test_nwcohesion.do`, plus `test_nwclique.do`/`test_nwnclique.do`/`test_nwnclan.do`/`test_nwkplex.do` (checked given their shared `unw_core.do` file, though none of them call the functions touched by this unit) - all pass clean in both dev mode and production mode. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 109: two real, user-reported nwergm bugs - a stray-integer display leak, and `estat gof`/`nwergm simulate` completely broken above ~16 nodes

User-reported directly, with a real console transcript: (1) `nwergm flomarriage, edges nodematch(seat) gwesp(0.5) absdist(wealth)` printed a run of bare, unexplained integers ("1", then "1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8" two or three times) before its own results table; (2) `estat gof` failed outright with "too many tokens", r(3000). Both root-caused to exact statements, not patched around.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Bug 1 root cause: `st_addvar()` returns the new variable's column index, and several call sites called it bare (uncaptured)** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Mata auto-displays any uncaptured top-level expression's value - confirmed directly, including the non-obvious fact that this applies to bare statements *inside* a called function's own body too, not just literal top-level `mata: expr` lines (a minimal repro: a function containing a bare loop-counter reference echoes every value when called, even nested). Localized via marker-based bisection (inserting `printf()` breadcrumbs into a working copy of the source and rebuilding) rather than static reading alone, since the actual call site (`ErgmNativeSetup()`'s own bare invocation in `nwergm.ado`, and `st_addvar()`'s own bare calls inside `ErgmNativeSampleCore()` in `unw_ergm.do`) turned out to be several function-call layers away from where the numbers appeared to originate. Fixed by capturing every previously-bare return value into a discard variable. The identical bug already existed in `calculate_betweenness_native()` (`unw_core.do`) - merely invisible there because `nwbetween.ado`'s own call site happens to sit inside a `qui foreach` block (confirmed directly: calling the same Mata method outside any `qui` context reproduces "1 2 3" immediately) - fixed there too, not left as a latent bug waiting for the next caller that isn't wrapped in `qui`. |
| **Bug 2 root cause: rendering a dense adjacency matrix as a literal Stata matrix-expression STRING and handing it to `nwset`'s own `mat()` option hits a hard, previously-undocumented Stata command-line "too many tokens" limit** | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Bisected precisely: a 15x15 literal matrix expression (225 comma-separated elements) parses cleanly; an otherwise-identical 16x16 one (256 elements) fails with r(3000) on the exact same command shape, confirmed via a minimal repro completely independent of nwergm. This is not a "large network" edge case - the florentine `flomarriage` network the user's own report used has exactly 16 nodes, meaning `estat gof` (and, found during the same investigation, `nwergm simulate`, which shares the identical pattern) was completely non-functional for that network and any other of comparable or larger size, not merely slow. Fixed by passing the matrix as a bare MATA VARIABLE NAME instead of a literal expression string - `nwset`'s own `mat()` option already accepts this form directly and evaluates it as Mata code rather than re-tokenizing a giant string as a Stata command line, so there is no size limit left to hit; confirmed this is already an established, working pattern elsewhere in this package (`nwrandom.ado`'s own generators pass their freshly-built matrices to `nwset, mat()` as bare Mata variable names, never as literal strings). All three call sites fixed: `nwergm_estat.ado`'s observed-network and simulated-network branches of `estat gof`, and `nwergm.ado`'s own `nwergm simulate` loop. |
| **Correctness, verified directly** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Re-ran the user's own exact reported command after each fix: the stray-integer output is gone and the reported coefficients are byte-identical to the pre-fix run (confirming a pure display fix, no behavior change). `estat gof` on the exact `flomarriage` network now produces a real, sensible goodness-of-fit table (including a `-1.0000` observed average geodesic, which is the package's own documented "disconnected network" convention, correctly reflecting that `flomarriage` genuinely has an isolate in the classical Florentine marriage data - not a new bug). |
| **Regression test added for the size limit specifically** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Every pre-existing assertion in `cscripts/test_nwergm_gof.do` used a 5-node network - comfortably under the ~16-node boundary, which is exactly why this bug shipped undetected. Added a new case using an 18-node random network (past the discovered boundary) exercising both `estat gof` and `nwergm simulate` end to end. |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | All 23 `cscripts/test_nwergm*.do` files plus `test_nwbetween.do`/`test_nwgraph_native.do` (blast radius of the `calculate_betweenness_native()` fix) pass clean in both dev mode and production mode. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 110: `nwissymmetric` sparsified (first of the `nwtomata`-dependent family)

Continuing the open items from `docs/PERFORMANCE_BENCHMARKS.md`'s own priority list (unit 103): the `nwtomata`-dependent family (`nwcloseness`, `nwburt`, `nwconstraint`, `nwissymmetric`, `nwsimilar`, `nwdissimilar`, `nwqap`) all materialize a full dense n-by-n matrix regardless of network sparsity. `nwissymmetric` is the simplest of the group - a single symmetry check - and closes first.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Sparse symmetry check** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `nwissymmetric.ado` called `nwtomata` to materialize the full dense matrix, then Mata's own `issymmetric()` - O(n^2) regardless of tie count. Added `check_issymmetric()` (`unw_core.do`): a network is symmetric iff every stored directed edge (i,j,w) has a matching reverse edge (j,i) with the identical weight - checked directly from the sparse edge list (`edgelist()`), O(m) edges times O(degree) per lookup, short-circuiting on the first asymmetric edge found. |
| **Correctness, verified against the old dense implementation** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | 300 random directed/undirected, binary/valued networks (n=3-25) cross-checked against Mata's own `issymmetric()` on the equivalent dense matrix - zero mismatches, including cases where the tie *pattern* is symmetric but a *weight* differs (confirmed the fix compares weights, not just presence). Zero prior test coverage existed for this command at all - new `cscripts/test_nwissymmetric.do` covers directed/undirected/valued/empty/one-sided-tie cases. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | n=10,000: previously excluded from the benchmark tier entirely (dense-matrix dependent) - now **0.562 seconds**. |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | New test passes clean in both dev mode and production mode; no other `.ado` file calls `nwissymmetric` internally (doc "See also" mentions only). |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 111: `nwburt` sparsified (second of the `nwtomata`-dependent family)

Continuing unit 110's series on the `nwtomata`-dependent family. `nwburt`'s default output (effective size, efficiency, constraint, hierarchy - one summary value per node) turns out not to actually need the two O(n^3) dense matrix products (`net*net`, `p*p`) its original formulas compute in full: both products are immediately masked by an element-wise multiply against the sparse tie matrix itself (`net :* net2`, `net :* (c:*c)`), so only entries at *actual edges* are ever used. This enables an exact sparse reformulation - the same "local induced subgraph" idiom already used for `calculate_clustering()`/`calculate_brokerage()` - computing each node's four measures directly from its own neighbor set, O(sum of out-degree^2) instead of O(n^3).

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Sparse effective size / efficiency / constraint / hierarchy** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Added `calculate_burt()` and a small `edge_weight_row()` helper to `unw_core.do`'s `NWdef` class: for each node `i`, loops only over `i`'s own neighbors `j`, and for each `j` loops only over `i`'s *other* neighbors `q` to accumulate the two-step-via-q terms - exactly the entries the original dense formulas' masking multiply would have kept, computed directly instead of filtered out of a full matrix product. `nwburt.ado`'s default (no `dyadredundancy`/`dyadconstraint` option) path now calls this instead of `nwtomata` + the dense `dyadicredundancy()`/`dyadicconstraint()`/`hierarchy()` functions. The `dyadredundancy`/`dyadconstraint` options (which ask for the full dyadic *networks* themselves, not just the four per-node summaries) are a disclosed, deliberate scope limit left on the original dense path unchanged - a materially different, less commonly used output shape. |
| **A formula-reading catch made proactively, before any test run**: the hierarchy computation's `z` term needs an explicit extra `net_ij` factor beyond the one already embedded in the dyadic-constraint value | ✅ (caught pre-test) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | The original dense formula is `z = rowsum(net :* (dc :/ avgc) :* log(dc :/ avgc))` - `net_ij` (i.e. `w_ij`) appears twice: once explicitly, once already folded inside `dc`. Manual derivation of the sparse per-edge accumulator during implementation caught this double-counting requirement before it could surface as a silent numerical mismatch. |
| **Cross-validation methodology note**: `.ado`-file-embedded, file-scope Mata functions (`nwburt.ado`'s own `dyadicredundancy()`/`dyadicconstraint()`/`hierarchy()`) could not be called directly from an external validation script even after triggering `nwburt`'s own execution path that uses them - confirmed as a general property of this codebase's `.ado` autoload semantics (the same behavior reproduces for `nwergm.ado`'s analogous `ergm_bridge_from_netobj()`), not a testing mistake. Worked around with an independent reimplementation of the reference formulas directly in the validation script, arguably a more rigorous check than reusing the original's own helpers would have been. | n/a (methodology) | n/a | n/a | ✅ (noted for future units hitting the same family) | |
| **Correctness, verified against an independent reference reimplementation** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | 200 random directed, variously-weighted networks (n=4-24) compared against a from-scratch reimplementation of the original dense formulas (not the original functions themselves, per the methodology note above) - max deviation across all four measures and every test was 9e-7, floating-point noise. Two genuine bugs were found and fixed in the *validation script* itself during this process, not in `calculate_burt()`: Mata has no implicit bridging of Stata dataset variables into bare expressions (`mata: (_effsize, ...)` silently resolves as "variable not found" even though the Stata variable genuinely exists - needs `st_data()`), and `nwset` allocates its own observations internally, so a preceding `qui set obs n` doubles the dataset instead of matching it. Existing `cscripts/test_nwburt.do` (star network, K4, isolate node, directed, empty network, `replace` guard, dyadredundancy/dyadconstraint dense-path regression) passes unmodified against the new default path. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | n=10,000 (50,087 edges, density 0.001): previously excluded from the benchmark tier entirely (dense triple matrix products infeasible at this scale) - now **2.39 seconds**. |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | Full `cscripts/` suite (143 files) re-run in dev mode after this change: 143/143 pass, zero regressions. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 112: `nwconstraint` sparsified (third of the `nwtomata`-dependent family)

Continuing the series (units 110/111). `nwconstraint` returns the full dyadic `c_ij` constraint matrix itself (a genuinely dense n-by-n output, unlike `nwburt`'s per-node summary) - so unlike the prior two units, the O(n^2) memory of the *result* is inherent to this command's own documented contract and was not the target here. The O(n^3) *compute* cost (two dense matrix products, `net*net`/`p*p`, in the command's own file-scope `constraint()` function) was the actual scaling problem, and - same as `nwburt` - is entirely avoidable: `c_ij` is only nonzero for a direct tie or a 2-hop path through a shared neighbor, so it can be built by walking each node's own neighborhood directly.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Sparse-compute dense dyadic constraint matrix** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Added `calculate_constraint_dyadic()` (`unw_core.do`, `NWdef` class, reusing `edge_weight_row()` from unit 111): for each node `i`, accumulates the direct term (`p_ij` for each neighbor `j`) and the indirect term (`p_iq*p_qj` for each neighbor `q` and each of `q`'s own neighbors `j`) directly into a dense n-by-n accumulator, then squares and zeroes the diagonal - O(sum of degree^2) instead of O(n^3), returning the exact same dense matrix shape `nwconstraint.ado` has always returned. Confirmed as a genuinely *different* formula from `nwburt`'s own internal `dyadicconstraint()` (which carries an extra tie-weight factor) - not a shared function, matching this command's own help file's existing explicit warning that the two are not interchangeable. |
| **Correctness, verified against an independent reference reimplementation** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | 200 random directed, variously-weighted networks (n=4-24): max deviation from a from-scratch reimplementation of the original dense formula was 1.7e-16 (floating-point noise). Existing `cscripts/test_nwconstraint.do` (star/directed-star/weighted-star, hand-derived exact fractions) passes unmodified. A genuinely new Mata syntax gotcha surfaced while writing the validation script: a single-line `mata: for(...) for(...) if(...) stmt` (no braces) fails with "unexpected end of line"/r(3000) when issued as a standalone `mata:`-prefixed Stata command line, even though the identical code runs fine inside a multi-line `mata:`/`end` block - worked around by using a vectorized `_diag()`+elementwise-subtract comparison instead of a manual nested-loop scan. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | n=10,000 (50k edges, density 0.001): previously excluded from the benchmark tier entirely (dense triple matrix products infeasible at this scale) - now **2.02 seconds**, including building and storing the full dense 10,000x10,000 result network. |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | Full `cscripts/` suite (143 files) re-run in dev mode after this change: 143/143 pass, zero regressions. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 113: `nwsimilar`/`nwdissimilar`/`nwcorrelate_nodes` vectorized (fourth/fifth of the `nwtomata`-dependent family) - plus two independent crash bugs found and fixed

Continuing the series (units 110-112). `nwsimilar`/`nwdissimilar` return a full dyadic similarity/dissimilarity matrix (like `nwconstraint`, a genuinely dense output) across up to 5 formula variants each (`type()`) and 3 modes each (`mode()`/`context()`) - all implemented as an explicit O(n^2)-pair loop that itself does an O(n) vector comparison per pair, O(n^3) total. `nwsimilar`'s own default `type(pearson)` delegates to `nwcorrelate_nodes` (`correlate_nodes()`, `unw_core.do`), almost certainly the single most commonly hit case of the whole `nwtomata`-dependent family - included in this same unit given how directly it shares the fix.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`nwsimilar`'s 4 explicit types vectorized** (`matches`/`jaccard`/`hamming`/`crossproduct`, all 3 modes) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Every formula reduces to each node's own binarized in/out-degree plus a shared-neighbor co-occurrence count (`Bout*Bout'`/`Bout'*Bout` - a single BLAS matrix product, not a loop) and a small per-pair correction for the original's own "mask positions i,j to 0 rather than remove them" convention (worked out by hand: since a network's own diagonal is always 0, masking only ever changes the comparison at the OTHER node's own position, a correction of exactly `Bout[i,j]+Bout[j,i]`). Confirmed directly that a 10,000x10,000 dense BLAS matrix multiply takes well under a second on this machine - these formulas' own remaining cost is ordinary memory bandwidth, not interpreted-loop overhead. |
| **`nwdissimilar`'s 4 vectorizable types** (`euclidean`/`nonmatches`/`jaccard`/`hamming`) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `euclidean` (operates on raw, non-binarized tie values) uses the analogous sum-of-squares/dot-product identity (`\|\|a-b\|\|^2 = \|\|a\|\|^2 - 2a.b + \|\|b\|\|^2`); the other three are `1 -`/`n -` the identical formulas from `nwsimilar`'s own fix. `manhatten` (sum of ABSOLUTE differences over raw, non-binarized values) has no equivalent O(1)-per-pair closed form the way squared-difference/match-count do - left as the original O(n^3) loop, a small, disclosed scope limit (one of five `type()` values, not the default). |
| **Genuinely new Mata gotcha found while deriving these**: colon operators do not broadcast a column vector against a row vector into an outer-sum matrix | ✅ (found, worked around) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `v :+ v''` for a column `v` raises a conformability error - confirmed directly, and confirmed this is specific to vector-against-vector (a vector broadcasting against a full n-by-n matrix works fine, used throughout both fixes). Worked around with a small `outersum()` helper using ordinary matrix multiplication (`v*J(1,n,1) + J(n,1,1)*v'`) instead. |
| **A masking bug caught during validation, before shipping**: `nwtomata`/`nwtomatafast` return the diagonal as missing (this package's own no-self-loop convention), not 0 - `. != 0` evaluates to true in Mata, so binarizing the raw matrix directly would have made every node incorrectly register a tie to itself. The original O(n^3) loop never hit this (it explicitly zeroed positions i,j as part of its own masking, independently of the diagonal's stored value). Fixed by zeroing the diagonal of a local copy before binarizing in both files' shared primitives. | ✅ (caught in testing) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Caught via `cscripts/test_nwsimilar.do`'s own pre-existing assertions failing on first run against the real `nwsimilar` command path (a hand-built validation script using literal 0-diagonal matrices had not exercised this - a validation-methodology gap now fixed, see the general note below). |
| **`correlate_nodes()` vectorized** (backs `nwcorrelate_nodes`/`nwcorrelate` on a single network, and `nwsimilar`'s own default `type(pearson)`) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Pearson correlation of two length-m vectors reduces to five sums (`m, Sx, Sy, Sxx, Syy, Sxy`) via the standard `r=(m*Sxy-Sx*Sy)/sqrt((m*Sxx-Sx^2)*(m*Syy-Sy^2))` identity; each sum needed only a small per-pair correction for the two removed positions (worked out by hand: since the diagonal is always 0, the cross term `Sxy` is entirely unaffected by removal, and the others each need one explicit subtraction) instead of the original's own O(n) `select()`-based removal per pair. The zero-variance fallback (used only when either compared vector is constant) is reproduced as a masked vectorized combination, verified not to leak `.` through arithmetic (confirmed directly that `. * 0` is still `.` in Mata, not 0 - a safe-division-then-mask pattern was used instead of a naive missing-value substitution). |
| **Two independent, pre-existing crash bugs found and fixed as a direct byproduct of rederiving `correlate_nodes()` from scratch** (not carried forward, since a branch that always crashes has no prior working behavior to stay compatible with) | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | (1) `outinboth==2` (`context(incoming)`) referenced `i_intvec`, a declared-but-never-assigned variable (a typo for `i_invec`) - an uninitialized operand, meaning `context(incoming)` could never have run successfully. (2) `outinboth==3` (`context(both)`) computed its own concatenated-vector length via `cols()` on a column vector - which is unconditionally 1, regardless of the vector's real length - causing a hard conformability error for any network with more than 3 nodes; reproduced directly against the untouched original. Both are now exercised for the first time by a new `cscripts/test_nwcorrelate.do` case, confirming `context(incoming)`/`context(both)` run cleanly and that `context(incoming)` gives a genuinely different result from `context(outgoing)` on an asymmetric directed network (not silently reusing the wrong direction). |
| **A separate, NOT-fixed quirk found and deliberately left as-is (bug-compatible)**: all three `outinboth` branches' own zero-variance fallback computes its cmin/cmax from the OUTGOING sums specifically, even inside the incoming-only and both-directions branches | ✅ (found, documented) | n/a | n/a | ✅ | Almost certainly a copy-paste artifact from the outgoing branch. Unlike the two bugs above, this branch was not crashing - there is a real prior behavior to stay compatible with for a performance-only pass, so it was reproduced exactly rather than "fixed" as a silent behavior change. Flagged here for a future unit to evaluate on its own (Pending). |
| **Correctness, verified against independent references** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `nwsimilar`'s 4 types and `nwdissimilar`'s 4 types: cross-validated against their own original O(n^3) implementations (kept in git history, extracted and renamed for comparison) across 200 random directed/valued networks (n=4-24) with a missing diagonal - exact match (0 max deviation) for every type/mode combination. `correlate_nodes()`: cross-validated against an independently corrected reimplementation (fixing the two crash bugs above but keeping the same O(n^3) select()-based algorithm, since the untouched original cannot even run for 2 of its 3 modes) across 100 random networks - max deviation 7.8e-16 (floating-point noise). All pre-existing test files (`test_nwsimilar.do`, `test_nwdissimilar.do`, `test_nwhierarchy.do` - a downstream caller of `nwdissimilar`'s default path) pass unmodified. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | n=10,000 (50k edges, density 0.001): previously excluded from the benchmark tier entirely. `nwsimilar` (matches/jaccard/hamming/crossproduct): 6.3-7.8s each. `nwdissimilar` (euclidean/nonmatches/jaccard/hamming): 6.9-7.9s each. `nwsimilar`'s own default, `type(pearson)` via `correlate_nodes()`: 22.5s (five sums instead of one, plus the fallback-mask overhead, hence somewhat slower than the others). |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | Full `cscripts/` suite (143 files, now 144 with the new `nwcorrelate` case) re-run in dev mode after this change: 143/143 (144/144) pass, zero regressions. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 114: `nwtriads` given a genuine subquadratic algorithm, not just vectorized

Unlike units 110-113 (all fixable by replacing an explicit interpreted loop with the identical computation via BLAS-backed matrix operations), `nwtriads`'s own dense formula could not be rescued that way: its core intermediate, `Ecompl` (the complement of "any tie exists"), is dense even for a very sparse network - almost every pair of nodes is non-adjacent - so every one of its matrix powers (including two genuine TRIPLE products) is unavoidably an O(n^3) dense computation regardless of how it's expressed. Confirmed directly: at n=10,000 this did not complete within several minutes and consumed multiple GB of RAM. A genuinely different algorithm was needed - the same core idea as Batagelj & Mrvar's (2001) subquadratic triad census.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Two-pass sparse triad census** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Added `calculate_triadcensus()` (rewritten in `unw_core.do`), plus two small new helper methods, `dyadstate()` (the 4-way null/asymmetric-forward/asymmetric-backward/mutual state of a dyad) and `connected_neighbors()` (union of in- and out-neighbors). Pass A counts triads with exactly ONE tied dyad: for every tied pair `(i,j)`, the number of third vertices with no tie to either is `n - cdeg(i) - cdeg(j) + \|connected_neighbors(i) ∩ connected_neighbors(j)\|` by inclusion-exclusion - the astronomically more numerous "which k" enumeration is never actually performed. Pass B counts triads with two or three tied dyads via the same "walk each node's own neighborhood" idiom as `calculate_burt()`/`calculate_constraint_dyadic()` (units 111/112): for each vertex `v` and each unordered pair of its own connected neighbors, classify the full triad - a 2-tied-dyad triad has a unique "hub" vertex and is found exactly once this way; an all-3-tied-dyad triad is found once per vertex acting as hub (3 times), so those types' final tally is divided by 3. The astronomically numerous all-null (`003`) count is never enumerated at all - obtained by subtracting the sum of the other 15 (all now cheap to compute) from `C(n,3)`, a closed form. |
| **Classification via a lookup table built directly from the verified-correct prior implementation, not re-derived from memory** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Classifying a triad's exact MAN type from its 3 dyad states (64 combinations: 4 states each, for 3 dyads) has several easily-confused, similarly-named directional sub-types (`021D`/`021U`/`021C`, `120D`/`120U`/`120C`, etc.) - rather than risk misremembering the naming convention while re-deriving this from first principles, the lookup table was instead built empirically: the OLD dense formula (proven correct by this package's own pre-existing tests) was evaluated on all 64 minimal 3-node cases and its own output recorded as ground truth, with zero risk of a convention mix-up. |
| **Correctness, verified against the original dense formula** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | 100 random directed and undirected networks (n=4-40): all 16 counts matched exactly (max deviation 4.5e-13, floating-point noise) against the untouched original (kept in git history). Existing `cscripts/test_nwtriads.do` passes unmodified. A genuinely new Mata gotcha found while writing the validation script: `new` is a reserved word - `mata: new = ...` fails with a parse error ("found where almost anything else expected") that gives no hint it's a reserved-identifier problem. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | n=10,000, density 0.001 (50k edges): previously did not complete within several minutes - now **2.97s** (undirected), **7.25s** (directed, more dyad-state work per pair). A denser case (0.5% density, 250k edges, avg degree 50) - 5x the standard benchmark-tier density - still completes in **61.7s**, scaling as expected with Pass B's own `sum of degree^2` term. |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | Full `cscripts/` suite (143 files) re-run in dev mode after this change: 143/143 pass, zero regressions. `nwtriads`'s own `transitivity` result is computed via `nwclustering` (already sparse since unit 104), untouched by this unit. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 115: `nwclique` given a genuine sparse Bron-Kerbosch, closing the last open item from the original n=10,000 performance pass

Closes the largest remaining item flagged since unit 107: `BronKerbosch()`'s R/P/X sets were dense 0/1 indicator vectors over ALL n nodes at every recursion level, so even a recursive call whose true local candidate set was tiny (bounded by degree, deep in the search tree) still paid O(n) per vector operation - confirmed as the reason `nwclique` hit 28GB+ RAM and did not complete at n=10,000 on a sparse (avg-degree-10) graph, even after unit 107's own pivoting fix (which helps moderately dense graphs but cannot shrink the top-level branching factor for a graph this sparse).

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Sparse Bron-Kerbosch with pivoting** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Added `BronKerboschSparse()` (`unw_core.do`) - the identical algorithm as `BronKerbosch()` (same pivot rule, same maximality invariant), but P/R/X are variable-length LISTS of actual node ids instead of length-n bitvectors, and neighbor lookups go through `connected_neighbors()` (added for `nwtriads`, unit 114 - the same "tied in either direction" symmetrized relation `calculate_cliques()` itself already needed) rather than a dense row. Set intersection (`P intersect N(v)`, the operation dominating this algorithm's own cost) uses a single SHARED marker array reused across the whole recursion, explicitly marked/unmarked around each localized use rather than reallocated - so its cost is bounded by the size of what's being marked (a candidate list, itself bounded by degree), never by n. Scoped narrowly to `nwclique.ado`'s own use (ordinary direct-tie cliques) - the original `BronKerbosch()` is left untouched, since `nwnclique.ado`/`nwnclan.ado`'s n-clique family calls it on a genuinely different "neighbor" relation (n-step reachability, not direct ties), which `connected_neighbors()` cannot substitute for. |
| **A genuinely new Mata `select()` gotcha, confirmed directly via a minimal repro**: it returns a malformed 0-by-0 matrix (not the expected 0-by-1) specifically when the INPUT has exactly one row and the filter keeps none of it - a 0-by-1 result at every other input size | ✅ (found, worked around) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Every `select()` call that could plausibly filter a single-element P/X/neighbor list down to nothing needed its result normalized back to a genuine 0-by-1 colvector via a small `fixcol()` helper, or a later `rows()`/indexing operation on it failed unpredictably several recursion levels deeper - exactly the kind of failure that would be very difficult to root-cause without a minimal, isolated repro (found by inserting `printf`-style tracing directly into a standalone copy of the recursion and comparing against a hand-derivation of the expected search tree, the same methodology used throughout this session). |
| **A second, independently significant performance bug caught by direct measurement before shipping, not assumed correctness-neutral**: the first working version of `BronKerboschSparse()` still returned a dense n-wide 0/1 row per call and concatenated results back up the call tree with `\` at every recursion level - reintroducing the exact "repeated dense concatenation" antipattern this codebase had already root-caused and fixed once before, for `nwgeodesic` (unit 106) | ✅ (found, fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Confirmed directly: this version still exhausted 26GB+ RAM and did not complete within 2 minutes at n=10,000, despite the sparse P/X/R representation working correctly on every smaller test - the sparse *search* was fixed, but the *reporting* path silently reintroduced the same class of quadratic-copying bug at the output side. Fixed by accumulating found cliques into a shared, growing STRING colvector (one compact "member id list" string appended per leaf, passed by reference through the whole recursion, confirmed directly that Mata's pass-by-reference semantics support this exact append-in-place-across-calls pattern) instead of a dense row - the dense n-wide output matrix `nwclique.ado`'s own contract expects is now built exactly once, from these strings, after the whole search finishes, not re-copied at every intermediate recursion level. |
| **Correctness, verified against the original dense `BronKerbosch()` path** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | 300 random directed/undirected, sparse-to-dense graphs (n=4-25, density 15-55%): the exact SET of maximal cliques found (compared as sorted bitmask-encoded rows, not merely counts) matched the untouched original in every single case. Existing `cscripts/test_nwclique.do` passes unmodified. |
| **Measured net effect** | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | n/a | n=10,000, avg degree 10 (50k edges): previously did not complete (28GB+ RAM, killed) - now **21.6 seconds**. n=5,000, avg degree 10: **5.6 seconds**. |
| Scoped regression sweep | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | n/a | Full `cscripts/` suite (143 files) re-run in dev mode after this change: 143/143 pass, zero regressions. `nwnclique.ado`/`nwnclan.ado` untouched (still use the original dense `BronKerbosch()` on their own n-step-reachability relation) - their own tests pass unaffected. |

## Harmonisation phase, unit 116: four related `nwset` lifecycle/collision bugs, all user-reported with exact root-cause analysis

All four items in this unit were reported directly by the user, mid-session, with precise line numbers, root-cause diagnoses, and (for the first two) a suggested fix - not found via this session's own testing. All four sit in the same area: what happens when a network name already exists, or doesn't exist at all.

| Feature | Implemented | Tested | Certified | Documented | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`nwset`'s `clear`/`nwclear` options used to `exit` unconditionally, even when the same call also carried a creation request** - e.g. `nwset ego alter, edgelist name(mini) nwclear` silently wiped the registry and returned without ever creating `mini`, with no error at all | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | User-reported with an exact fix already sketched: guard the early exits to fire only when clearing is the *only* thing requested; otherwise clear as a side effect and fall through to the normal creation path. Implemented with one refinement beyond the sketch: the clear/`nwclear` side effect had to run *before* the pre-existing `nws_create()` step, not after - `nwclear` itself drops the whole `nw` Mata registry object, so running it after `nws_create()` had already built a fresh one would immediately destroy it again (confirmed directly via a `mat()`-based repro that crashed until reordered). Combining `nwclear` with a varlist-based form (edgelist/twomode/temporal) is not made to work by this fix - `nwclear` wipes the Stata dataset those forms read their own source columns from, so the two are inherently in tension; that combination now surfaces as an ordinary "variable not found" error instead of a silent no-op, which is what this fix actually guarantees (not making an incoherent combination succeed). `mat()` is unaffected (a Mata expression, not dataset columns) and now works correctly combined with either option. |
| **A second, independent, adjacent bug found while fixing the first: `nwset, clear` (and `nw2set`'s identical form) has *always* been completely broken**, calling `nwdrop _all, netonly` - `netonly` is not, and has never been, an option `nwdrop.ado` accepts (confirmed directly: its own syntax line only declares `clean`) | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Every call to the standalone `nwset, clear` form errored ("option netonly not allowed", r(198)) before this fix - not a corner case, the *only* documented use of `clear` at all. Root-caused: plain `nwdrop _all` (no option) already does exactly what `clear` is documented to do (drop all networks, leave the Stata dataset untouched, confirmed directly) - `netonly` was pure vestigial cruft, not a missing feature, likely left over from an earlier `nwdrop.ado` that once had such an option. Fixed by removing the invalid option from all three call sites (`nwset.ado` x2, `nw2set.ado` x1). |
| **`nwset`'s `overwrite` option was declared in the syntax line but never actually connected to any check anywhere in the file** - a network name collision on an *explicitly* requested `name()` always silently auto-picked a different valid name via `nw_validate` and warned, regardless of whether `overwrite` was given, leaving the caller's own name silently pointing at stale data | ✅ (fixed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | User-reported, comparing this against `nwgenerate`'s own create/replace convention (`nwgenerate friendship = ...` errors on a name collision unless `replace` is given) and asking for the same discipline here, with `replace` as the primary option name (`overwrite` kept as a backward-compatible alias).
